The **Uyghurs**,[^cite_note-28] alternatively spelled **Uighurs**,[^cite_note-Merriam-Webster_Uighur-29][^cite_note-Collins_Uighur-30][^cite_note-31] **Uygurs**, or **Uigurs**, are a [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) ethnic group originating from and culturally affiliated with the general region of [Central Asia](6742.md) and [East Asia](19605700.md). They speak [Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_language), a [Karluk language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karluk_languages) that shares the same origin with [Uzbek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbek_language). The Uyghurs are recognized as the [titular nationality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titular_nation) of the [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) Uyghur Autonomous Region in [Northwest China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_China). They are one of [China's 55 officially recognized ethnic minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_minorities_in_China).[^cite_note-Celtic-32]

The Uyghurs are genetically related to West Eurasians, East Asians, Siberians, South Asians and can trace their ancestry to various peoples and cultures, such as the [Tocharians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocharians), [Uyghur Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khanate), [Qocho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qocho), [Kara-Khanid Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara-Khanid_Khanate), and [Qara Khitai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qara_Khitai), that had inhabited or migrated to the region. They were historically influenced and often ruled by neighboring [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_China), [Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire), [Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Empire), and Turkic civilizations. Originally followers of many belief systems, inhabitants of the region were gradually [converted to Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara-Khanid_Khanate#Conversion_to_Islam_\(c._934_CE\)) after the 10th century, especially under the [Chagatai Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_Khanate#Moghulistan_\(1363%E2%80%931487\)). By the 16th century, most of them identified as [Muslims](19541.md) and no longer recognized symbols of their [Buddhist past](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Buddhism#Spread_to_Central_Asia). [Islam](6037917.md) continues to play an important role in Uyghur culture and identity.

Prior to the 20th century, the Uyghurs did not have a common unifying ethnonym. The name "Uyghur" was used to refer to the non-Muslim inhabitants of Qocho, mainly Buddhists, who resisted conversion to Islam until the 16th century. The name fell out of use afterward and the region inhabited by the modern Uyghur people came under the rule of the [Qing dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty) following the [Dzungar–Qing Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungar%E2%80%93Qing_Wars) in the 18th century. Settlers of various ethnicities were relocated to [Dzungaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungaria), which was later combined with the [Tarim Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarim_Basin) to the south to become [Xinjiang Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_under_Qing_rule).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDillon200424-33][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladney2004213-34]

Until 1921, the inhabitants of the Tarim Basin identified themselves by terms such as *yerlik* ("locals"), *Musulman khalq* ("Muslim people"), or by regional designations including *Kashgarlik* and *Yarkandlik*. In the twentieth century, under the influence of movements advocating autonomy, the need for a common ethnic designation emerged. In a conference held by the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), Turkic intellectuals from all over the Tarim Basin gathered in [Tashkent](57533.md) and adopted the ethnonym "Uyghur" for their people, although they were aware of the differences between the modern and ancient Uyghurs. The extent of the Uyghur Khaganate's territory did not reach the entire area inhabited by modern Uyghurs such as western [Kashgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yettishar). Later Chinese governments continued to use the name "Uyghur" as an umbrella category for the people of the Tarim Basin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESultanov201787-35][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBellér-Hann200850-51-36][^cite_note-East-West-Center-Washington-2005-37]

More Uyghurs live in the Tarim Basin than in northern Xinjiang.[^cite_note-38] Several thousand reside in [Taoyuan, Hunan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoyuan_County#Taoyuan_Uyghurs).[^cite_note-english.people.com.cn-39] Significant [diasporic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaspora) Uyghur communities exist in Turkic countries such as [Kazakhstan](16642.md), [Kyrgyzstan](170131.md), [Uzbekistan](31853.md), and [Turkey](11125639.md),[^cite_note-40] in addition to smaller ones elsewhere.[^cite_note-41]

Since 2014,[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44] the [Chinese government](23239.md) has been accused of subjecting Uyghurs in Xinjiang to [widespread persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Uyghurs_in_China), including [arbitrary arrest and detention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_internment_camps),[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47] [forced sterilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_sterilization),[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-Falconer-49] and [forced labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labor).[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] The Chinese government denies the accusations.

## Ethnonym

### Historical ethnonyms

The [Karluk Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karluk_languages) speaking group in modern-day Xinjiang lacked well-established ethnic endonyms before the 20th century. They would simply identify themselves as "Muslims"[^cite_note-kim-53][^cite_note-Ho-dong-Kim-2004-54][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillward2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA93_93\]-55] or "Sarts" (meaning merchant, trader, or [caravan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caravan_\(travellers\)) leader),[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57] or by their originating oasis such as "Kashgari" for people from Kashgar or "Hotani" for people from Hotan.[^cite_note-brophy-58][^cite_note-kim-53]

From the 18th to early 20th century, Turkic Muslims in Xinjiang was commonly referred to as **Chanhui** (Chinese: [缠回](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E7%BC%A0%E5%9B%9E)) or **[Chantou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chantuu) Huihui** (Chinese: [缠头回回](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E7%BC%A0%E5%A4%B4%E5%9B%9E%E5%9B%9E)) (still officially used in Mongolia), which is a Chinese exonym literally meaning turban-wearing Muslims, to be distinguished from Chinese Muslims (nowadays [Hui people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people)), commonly referred to as Hanhui (Chinese: [汉回](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E6%B1%89%E5%9B%9E)), literally meaning Han Chinese Muslims.[^cite_note-59] Although the term "Hui (Chinese: [回](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E5%9B%9E))" or "Huihui (Chinese: [回回](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E5%9B%9E%E5%9B%9E))" originates from the term "Uyghur" (Chinese: 回鹘; *huí hú*), which refers to a [Siberian Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberian_Turkic_languages) speaking, instead of [Karluk Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karluk_languages) speaking, ethnic group speaking the "[Old Uyghur language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Uyghur)", who established the [Uyghur Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate), [Qocho Uyghur Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qocho), and [Ganzhou Uyghur Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganzhou_Uyghur_Kingdom), the term was later applied to people who believe in any of the [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions), such as [Judaist Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaifeng_Jews) (蓝帽回回; *lán mào huí huí*), [Christian Ossetians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ossetians) (绿睛回回; *lǜ jīng huí huí*), [Muslim Chams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chams) (占城回回; *zhàn chéng huí huí*), *etc.* during the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire),[^cite_note-60] and gradually applied specifically to people who believe in Islam, such as [Hui people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people) (回族; *huí zú*), [Baihui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bai_people#Islam) (白族回回; *bái zú huí huí*), [Menghui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menghui?action=edit\&redlink=1) (蒙古回回; *měng gǔ huí huí*), *etc.* until nowadays.[^cite_note-61]

Although the semantic meaning of the term "Hui (Chinese: [回](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E5%9B%9E))" shifted from the [Old Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Uyghur) speaking people to [Muslims](19541.md), the Old Uyghur speaking people themselves were predominately non-Muslims. None of [Uyghur Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate), [Qocho Uyghur Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qocho), and [Ganzhou Uyghur Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganzhou_Uyghur_Kingdom) established Islam as the official religion of their states, and their direct descendant, which was renamed as [Yugur people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugurs), speaking the [Neo-Uyghur language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Yugur_language) predominately practise Buddhism until nowadays, just like their [Old Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Uyghur) ancestors.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64] Nevertheless, due to this semantic connection, ethnonyms of three of the 56 officially recognised ethnic groups in the People's Republic of China, who are [Hui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people), [Yugurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugurs), and Uyghurs, share the same etymological origin.[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67] Although Yugurs and Uyghurs both speak Turkic languages, the [Yugur (Neo-Uyghur) language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Yugur_language), just like the [Old Uyghur language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Uyghur), belongs to the [Siberian Turkic branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberian_Turkic_languages), and [Uyghur language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_language) belongs to th [Karluk Turkic branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karluk_languages). The two ethnic groups are also genetically very distinct from each other.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69]

### Usage of the modern ethnonym "Uyghur"

A minority of the modern-day Uyghurs were descendants of the [Qocho Uyghur Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qocho) based in nowadays Eastern Xinjiang, who spoke the [Siberian Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberian_Turkic_languages) [Old Uyghur language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Uyghur) and predominately practised Buddhism.[^cite_note-70] They were linguistically, religiously and culturally assimilated to the vast majority of [Karluk Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karluk_languages) [Chagatai language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_language) speaking Muslims in the [Chagatai Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_Khanate) after the [Chagatai conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qocho#Conquest_by_Muslim_Chagatais).[^cite_note-GibbLewis19982-71][^cite_note-72] Since then, the Uyghur identity in the modern-day Xinjiang had perished until the term "Uyghur" was revived in 1921.[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74]

One thing worth to mention is that after the split of the [Eastern Chagatai Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moghulistan), its minor successor, the [Turpan Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turpan_Khanate) based in Eastern Xinjiang, was externally referred to as "Uyghurstan" due to Eastern Xinjiang's historical connection to the Qocho Uyghur Kingdom, even if the local population had started speaking the Karluk Turkic Chagatai language and practised Islam,[^cite_note-uyghurstan-75] whereas the major successor, [Yarkent Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarkent_Khanate) based in the Tarim basin, which later conquered the Turpan Khanate, was externally referred to as "Kashghar Khanate".[^cite_note-Bano-76]

The modern application of the term "Uyghur" to the [Karluk Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karluk_languages) speaking people was in fact a 20th century invention.[^cite_note-77] The Tashkent conference held in the Soviet Union in 1921 gathered Turkic Muslims from the Tarim Basin, who decided to use the term "Uyghur" as the group's modern ethnonym,[^cite_note-East-West-Center-Washington-2005-37] although they themselves also realised that their ethnicity should not be confused with that of the [Uyghur Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate) of the Medieval history.[^cite_note-Linda-Benson-1990-78] This usage was then formally accepted by the Soviet Union, and an [Uighur raion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uygur_District) was established in the modern-day Kazakhstan bordering China.[^cite_note-79] Hoping to divide the Muslim population of Xinjiang, the [Sheng Shicai ruled Xinjiang government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_Province,_Republic_of_China) in China later accepted the modern usage of the term "Uyghur" from the Soviet Union, but the group in Xinjiang was further divided to Uyghurs and [**Taranchis**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taranchi) (modern Uyghurs in [Ili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ili_Kazakh_Autonomous_Prefecture)) among the 14 ethnic groups recognised at the time.[^cite_note-80] After the establishment of the People's Republic of China, the Taranchis were officially counted into the Uyghurs.[^cite_note-81]

### Etymology of the term "Uyghur"

In the [Uyghur language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_language), the [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym) is written ئۇيغۇر in [Arabic script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Arabic_alphabet), Уйғур in [Uyghur Cyrillic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Cyrillic_alphabet) and *Uyghur* or *Uygur* (as the standard Chinese [romanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization), [GB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Standards_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) 3304–1991) in [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Latin_alphabet);[^cite_note-MairPrimer-82] they are all pronounced as [\[ʔʊjˈʁʊːr\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFairbankChʻen1968364-83][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEÖzoğlu200416-84] In [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language), this is [transcribed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcription_into_Chinese_characters) into [characters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_characters) as [维吾尔](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E7%BB%B4%E5%90%BE%E5%B0%94) / [維吾爾](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E7%B6%AD%E5%90%BE%E7%88%BE), which is [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Chinese) in [pinyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinyin) as *Wéiwú'ěr*.

In English, the name is officially spelled *Uyghur* by the [Xinjiang government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang#Politics)[^cite_note-85] but also appears as *Uighur*,[^cite_note-oed-86] *Uigur*[^cite_note-oed-86] and *Uygur* (these reflect the various Cyrillic spellings Уиғур, Уигур and Уйгур). The name is usually pronounced in English as [/ˈwiː.ɡʊər, -.ɡər/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*WEE-goor, -⁠gər*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key) (and thus may be preceded by the [indefinite article](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indefinite_article) "a"),[^cite_note-oed-86][^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88][^cite_note-Merriam-Webster_Uighur-29] although some Uyghurs advocate the use of a more native pronunciation [/ˌuː.i.ˈɡʊər/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*OO-ee-GOOR*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key) instead (which, in contrast, calls for the indefinite article "an").[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHahn20064-26][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrompp20057-27][^cite_note-89]

The term's original meaning is unclear. [Old Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Turkic) inscriptions record the word *uyɣur*[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERussell-Smith200533-90] (Old Turkic: 𐰆𐰖𐰍𐰆𐰺); an example is found on the Sudzi inscription, "I am khan ata of [Yaglaqar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaglakar_clan), came from the Uigur land." (Old Turkic: *Uyγur jerinte Yaγlaqar qan ata keltim*).[^cite_note-91] It is transcribed into [Tang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_dynasty) annals as [回纥](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E5%9B%9E%E7%BA%A5) / [回紇](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E5%9B%9E%E7%B4%87) (Mandarin: *Huíhé*, but probably \*\[ɣuɒiɣət] in [Middle Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Chinese)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackerras1968224-92] It was used as the name of one of the Turkic polities formed in [the interim between the First and Second Göktürk Khaganates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%B6kt%C3%BCrk_civil_war) (AD 630–684).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGüzel2002-93] The *[Old History of the Five Dynasties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_History_of_the_Five_Dynasties)* records that in 788 or 809, the Chinese acceded to a Uyghur request and emended their transcription to [回鹘](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E5%9B%9E%E9%B9%98) / [回鶻](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E5%9B%9E%E9%B6%BB) (Mandarin: *Huíhú*, but \[ɣuɒiɣuət] in Middle Chinese).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGolden1992155-94][^cite_note-95]

Modern [etymological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etymology) explanations for the name *Uyghur* range from derivation from the verb "follow, accommodate oneself"[^cite_note-oed-86] and adjective "non-rebellious" (i.e., from Turkic *uy/uð-*) to the verb meaning "wake, rouse or stir" (i.e., from Turkic *oðğur-*). None of these is thought to be satisfactory because the sound shift of /ð/ and /ḏ/ to /j/ does not appear to be in place by this time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGolden1992155-94] The etymology therefore cannot be conclusively determined and its referent is also difficult to fix. The "Huihe" and "Huihu" seem to be a political rather than a [tribal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe) designation[^cite_note-96] or it may be one group among several others collectively known as the [Toquz Oghuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toquz_Oghuz).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERussell-Smith200532-97] The name fell out of use in the 15th century, but was reintroduced in the early 20th century[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFairbankChʻen1968364-83][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEÖzoğlu200416-84] by the Soviet [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks) to replace the previous terms *[Turk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples)* and *Turki*.[^cite_note-98][^cite_note-turki-100] The name is currently used to refer to the settled Turkic urban dwellers and farmers of the [Tarim Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarim_Basin) who follow traditional Central Asian [sedentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedentism) practices, distinguishable from the nomadic Turkic populations in Central Asia.

The earliest record of a Uyghur tribe appears in accounts from the [Northern Wei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Wei) (4th–6th century A.D.), wherein they were named 袁紇 *Yuanhe* (< [MC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Chinese) [ZS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhengzhang_Shangfang) \**ɦʉɐn-ɦət*) and derived from a confederation named [高车](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E9%AB%98%E8%BD%A6) / [高車](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E9%AB%98%E8%BB%8A) (lit. "High Carts"), read as *Gāochē* in [Mandarin Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese) but originally with the reconstructed Middle Chinese pronunciation \*\[kɑutɕʰĭa], later known as the *[Tiele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiele_people)* ([铁勒](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E9%93%81%E5%8B%92) / [鐵勒](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E9%90%B5%E5%8B%92), *Tiělè*).[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMair2006137–8-103] *Gāochē* in turn has been connected to the [Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_language) *Qangqil* (قاڭقىل or Қаңқил).[^cite_note-104]

## Identity



A Uyghur *girde* [naan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naan) baker

Uyghur blacksmiths at work. [Yengisar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yengisar_County), [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang), China. Yengisar is famous for the quality of its knives.



Uyghur man in traditional clothing, playing a tambur, a traditional Uyghur instrument

Throughout its history, the term *Uyghur* has had an increasingly expansive definition. Initially signifying only a small coalition of [Tiele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiele_people) tribes in northern China, Mongolia and the [Altai Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altai_Mountains), it later denoted citizenship in the [Uyghur Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate). Finally, it was expanded into an ethnicity whose ancestry originates with the fall of the Uyghur Khaganate in the year 842, causing Uyghur migration from [Mongolia](19271.md) into the Tarim Basin. The Uyghurs who moved to the Tarim Basin mixed with the local [Tocharians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocharians), and converted to the Tocharian religion, and adopted their culture of oasis agriculture.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106] The fluid definition of *Uyghur* and the diverse ancestry of modern Uyghurs create confusion as to what constitutes true Uyghur [ethnography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnography) and [ethnogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnogenesis). Contemporary scholars consider modern Uyghurs to be the descendants of a number of peoples, including the ancient Uyghurs of Mongolia migrating into the Tarim Basin after the fall of the Uyghur Khaganate, Iranic [Saka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saka) tribes and other [Indo-European peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_migrations) inhabiting the Tarim Basin before the arrival of the Turkic Uyghurs.[^cite_note-xinjiang-107]

Uyghur activists identify with the [Tarim mummies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarim_mummies), the remains of an ancient population that inhabited the region, but genetic research on the mummies and their possible links to modern Uyghurs remains contentious, both for Chinese government officials concerned about ethnic separatism and for Uyghur activists who fear the findings could affect their indigenous claims.[^cite_note-108]

A genomic study published in 2021 found that these early mummies had high levels of [Ancient North Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_North_Eurasian) ancestry (ANE, about 72%), with smaller admixture from [Ancient Northeast Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Northeast_Asians) (ANA, about 28%), but no detectable [Western Steppe-related ancestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Steppe_Herder).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang2021"Using_qpAdm,_we_modeled_the_Tarim_Basin_individuals_as_a_mixture_of_two_ancient_autochthonous_Asian_genetic_groups:_the_ANE,_represented_by_an_Upper_Palaeolithic_individual_from_the_Afontova_Gora_site_in_the_upper_Yenisei_River_region_of_Siberia_(AG3)_(about_72%),_and_ancient_Northeast_Asians,_represented_by_Baikal_EBA_(about_28%)_(Supplementary_Data_1E_and_Fig._3a)._Tarim_EMBA2_from_Beifang_can_also_be_modeled_as_a_mixture_of_Tarim_EMBA1_(about_89%)_and_Baikal_EBA_(about_11%)."-109][^cite_note-110] They formed a genetically isolated local population that "adopted neighbouring pastoralist and agriculturalist practices, which allowed them to settle and thrive along the shifting riverine oases of the Taklamakan Desert."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang2021-111] These mummified individuals were long suspected to have been "[Proto-Tocharian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Tocharian)-speaking pastoralists", ancestors of the [Tocharians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocharians), but the authors of this study found no genetic connection with [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages)-speaking migrants, particularly the [Afanasievo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afanasievo) or [BMAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactria%E2%80%93Margiana_Archaeological_Complex) cultures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang2021"Our_results_do_not_support_previous_hypotheses_for_the_origin_of_the_Tarim_mummies,_who_were_argued_to_be_Proto-Tocharian-speaking_pastoralists_descended_from_the_Afanasievo,_or_to_have_originated_among_the_Bactria–Margiana_Archaeological_Complex_or_Inner_Asian_Mountain_Corridor_cultures._Instead,_although_Tocharian_may_have_been_plausibly_introduced_to_the_Dzungarian_Basin_by_Afanasievo_migrants_during_the_Early_Bronze_Age,_we_find_that_the_earliest_Tarim_Basin_cultures_appear_to_have_arisen_from_a_genetically_isolated_local_population_that_adopted_neighboring_pastoralist_and_agriculturalist_practices,_which_allowed_them_to_settle_and_thrive_along_the_shifting_riverine_oases_of_the_Taklamakan_Desert."-112] Zhang et al. (2025) investigated a Late Bronze Age site in the far west of the Tarim Basin, dated 1600 to 1400 BC. Its inhabitants overwhelmingly descended from the [Sintashta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sintashta_culture) and [Andronovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andronovo_culture) population, with additional ancestry from [BMAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactria%E2%80%93Margiana_Archaeological_Complex) (10%) and Tarim_EMBA (12%). Nearly all subjects belonged to [Y-DNA haplogroup R-M17.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_R-M17)[^cite_note-Zhang-2025-113]

### Origin of modern nomenclature

> The Uighurs are the people whom old Russian travelers called "[Sart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sart)" (a name they used for sedentary, Turkish-speaking Central Asians in general), while Western travelers called them Turki, in recognition of their language. The Chinese used to call them "Ch'an-t'ou" ('Turbaned Heads') but this term has been dropped, being considered derogatory, and the Chinese, using their own pronunciation, now called them Weiwuerh. As a matter of fact there was for centuries no 'national' name for them; people identified themselves with the oasis they came from, such as Kashgar or Turfan.

— Owen Lattimore, "Return to China's Northern Frontier." *The Geographical Journal*, Vol. 139, No. 2, June 1973[^cite_note-114]

The term "Uyghur" was not used to refer to a specific existing ethnicity in the 19th century: it referred to an 'ancient people'. A late-19th-century encyclopedia entitled *The Cyclopædia of India and of Eastern and Southern Asia* said "the Uigur are the most ancient of Turkish tribes and formerly inhabited a part of Chinese Tartary (Xinjiang), now occupied by a mixed population of Turk, Mongol and [Kalmuck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalmuck)".[^cite_note-115] Before 1921/1934, Western writers called the Turkic-speaking Muslims of the oases "Turki" and the Turkic Muslims who had migrated from the Tarim Basin to [Ili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ili_Kazakh_Autonomous_Prefecture), [Ürümqi](199155.md) and [Dzungaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungaria) in the northern portion of Xinjiang during the Qing dynasty were known as "[Taranchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taranchi)", meaning "farmer". The Russians and other foreigners referred to them as "Sart",[^cite_note-Benson1998-116] "Turk" or "Turki".[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-turki-100] In the early 20th century they identified themselves by different names to different peoples and in response to different inquiries: they called themselves Sarts in front of Kazakhs and Kyrgyz while they called themselves "Chantou" if asked about their identity after first identifying as a Muslim.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-brophy-58] The term "Chantou" (纏頭; *Chántóu*, meaning "Turban Head") was used to refer to the Turkic Muslims of [Altishahr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altishahr) (now [Southern Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Xinjiang)),[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] including by Hui (Tungan) people.[^cite_note-Forbes1986-121] These groups of peoples often identify themselves by their originating oasis instead of an ethnicity;[^cite_note-122] for example those from [Kashgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgar) may refer to themselves as Kashgarliq or [Kashgari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgari), while those from [Hotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotan) identity themselves as "Hotani".[^cite_note-brophy-58][^cite_note-kim-53] Other Central Asians once called all the inhabitants of Xinjiang's Southern oases Kashgari,[^cite_note-123] a term still used in some regions of Pakistan.[^cite_note-124] The Turkic people also used "Musulman", which means "Muslim", to describe themselves.[^cite_note-kim-53][^cite_note-Ho-dong-Kim-2004-54][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillward2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA93_93\]-55]



Mural fragment depicting two Uyghur donors, from the [Bezeklik Thousand Buddha Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bezeklik_Thousand_Buddha_Caves). Kept in the [Museum of Asian Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Asian_Art) in [Berlin](3354.md).

Rian Thum explored the concepts of identity among the ancestors of the modern Uyghurs in [Altishahr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altishahr) (the native Uyghur name for Eastern Turkestan or Southern Xinjiang) before the adoption of the name "Uyghur" in the 1930s, referring to them by the name "Altishahri" in his article *Modular History: Identity Maintenance before Uyghur Nationalism*. Thum indicated that Altishahri Turkis did have a sense that they were a distinctive group separate from the Turkic Andijanis to their west, the nomadic Turkic Kirghiz, the nomadic Mongol Qalmaq and the Han Chinese [Khitay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khitan_people) before they became known as Uyghurs. There was no single name used for their identity; various native names Altishahris used for identify were Altishahrlik (Altishahr person), yerlik (local), Turki and Musulmān (Muslim); the term Musulmān in this situation did not signify religious connotations, because the Altishahris exclude other Muslim peoples like the Kirghiz while identifying themselves as Musulmān.[^cite_note-Thum2012-125][^cite_note-126] Dr. Laura J Newby says the sedentary Altishahri Turkic people considered themselves separate from other Turkic Muslims since at least the 19th century.[^cite_note-127]

The name "Uyghur" reappeared after the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) took the 9th-century [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym) from the [Uyghur Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate), then reapplied it to all non-nomadic Turkic Muslims of Xinjiang.[^cite_note-Ildikó-Bellér-Hann-2007-128] It followed western European [orientalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orientalism) like [Julius Klaproth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Klaproth) in the 19th century who revived the name and spread the use of the term to local Turkic intellectuals[^cite_note-129] and a 19th-century proposal from Russian historians that modern-day Uyghurs were descended from the [Kingdom of Qocho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Qocho) and [Kara-Khanid Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara-Khanid_Khanate) formed after the dissolution of the Uyghur Khaganate.[^cite_note-Millward-130] Historians generally agree that the adoption of the term "Uyghur" is based on a decision from a 1921 conference in [Tashkent](57533.md), attended by Turkic Muslims from the Tarim Basin (Xinjiang).[^cite_note-Ildikó-Bellér-Hann-2007-128][^cite_note-East-West-Center-Washington-2005-37][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-Akiner2013-132] There, "Uyghur" was chosen by them as the name of their ethnicity, although they themselves note that they were not to be confused with the [Uyghur Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate) of medieval history.[^cite_note-Benson1998-116][^cite_note-Linda-Benson-1990-78] According to Linda Benson, the Soviets and their client [Sheng Shicai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheng_Shicai) intended to foster a Uyghur nationality to divide the Muslim population of Xinjiang, whereas the various Turkic Muslim peoples preferred to identify themselves as "Turki", "East Turkestani" or "Muslim".[^cite_note-Benson1998-116]

On the other hand, the ruling regime of China at that time, the [Kuomintang](16903.md), grouped all Muslims, including the Turkic-speaking people of Xinjiang, into the "[Hui nationality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Races_Under_One_Union)".[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134] The [Qing dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty) and the Kuomintang generally referred to the sedentary oasis-dwelling Turkic Muslims of Xinjiang as "turban-headed Hui" to differentiate them from other predominantly Muslim ethnicities in China.[^cite_note-Benson1998-116][^cite_note-135][^cite_note-137] In the 1930s, foreigners travelers in Xinjiang such as [George W. Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Hunter_\(missionary\)), [Peter Fleming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Fleming_\(writer\)), [Ella Maillart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ella_Maillart) and [Sven Hedin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sven_Hedin), referred to the Turkic Muslims of the region as "Turki" in their books. Use of the term Uyghur was unknown in Xinjiang until 1934. The area governor, [Sheng Shicai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheng_Shicai), came to power, adopting the Soviet ethnographic classification instead of the Kuomintang's and became the first to promulgate the official use of the term "Uyghur" to describe the Turkic Muslims of Xinjiang.[^cite_note-Benson1998-116][^cite_note-Millward-130][^cite_note-138] "Uyghur" replaced "rag-head".[^cite_note-139]

Sheng Shicai's introduction of the "Uighur" name for the Turkic people of Xinjiang was criticized and rejected by Turki intellectuals such as Pan-Turkist [Jadids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jadid) and [East Turkestan independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Turkestan_independence_movement) activists [Muhammad Amin Bughra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Amin_Bughra) (Mehmet Emin) and [Masud Sabri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masud_Sabri). They demanded the names "Türk" or "Türki" be used instead as the ethnonyms for their people. Masud Sabri viewed the [Hui people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people) as Muslim [Han Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Chinese) and separate from his people,[^cite_note-140] while Bughrain criticized Sheng for his designation of Turkic Muslims into different ethnicities which could sow disunion among Turkic Muslims.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillward2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA209_209\]-141][^cite_note-142] After the Communist victory, the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) under Chairman [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong) continued the Soviet classification, using the term "Uyghur" to describe the modern ethnicity.[^cite_note-Benson1998-116]

In current usage, *Uyghur* refers to settled Turkic-speaking urban dwellers and farmers of the [Tarim Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarim_Basin) and [Ili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ili_Kazakh_Autonomous_Prefecture) who follow traditional Central Asian sedentary practices, as distinguished from nomadic Turkic populations in Central Asia. However, Chinese government agents designate as "Uyghur" certain peoples with significantly divergent histories and ancestries from the main group. These include the Lopliks of [Ruoqiang County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruoqiang_County) and the [Dolan people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolan_people), thought to be closer to the [Oirat Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oirats) and the [Kyrgyz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyrgyz_people).[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144] The use of the term Uyghur led to anachronisms when describing the history of the people.[^cite_note-ReedRaschke2010-145] In one of his books, the term Uyghur was deliberately not used by James Millward.[^cite_note-Levey2006-146]

Another ethnicity, the [Western Yugur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugur) of [Gansu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gansu), identify themselves as the "Yellow Uyghur" (*Sarïq Uyghur*).[^cite_note-147] Some scholars say the Yugurs' culture, language and religion are closer to the original culture of the original Uyghur Karakorum state than is the culture of the modern Uyghur people of Xinjiang.[^cite_note-148] Linguist and ethnographer [S. Robert Ramsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S._Robert_Ramsey) argues for inclusion of both the Eastern and Western Yugur and the [Salar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salar_people) as sub-groups of the Uyghur based on similar historical roots for the Yugur and on perceived linguistic similarities for the Salar.[^cite_note-149]

"*Turkistani*" is used as an alternate ethnonym by some Uyghurs.[^cite_note-150] For example, the Uyghur diaspora in Arabia, adopted the identity "*Turkistani*". Some Uyghurs in Saudi Arabia adopted the Arabic [nisba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nisba_\(onomastics\)#Nisba_to_a_place) of their home city, such as "*Al-[Kashgari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgari)*" from [Kashgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgar). Saudi-born Uyghur [Hamza Kashgari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamza_Kashgari)'s family originated from Kashgar.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152]

## Population



Uyghur hunter in Kashgar

The Uyghur population within China generally remains centered in Xinjiang region with some smaller subpopulations elsewhere in the country, such as in [Taoyuan County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoyuan_County) where an estimated 5,000–10,000 live.[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-zhiyu-154]

The size of the Uyghur population, particularly in China, has been the subject of dispute. Chinese authorities place the Uyghur population within the Xinjiang region to be just over 12 million, comprising approximately half of the total regional population.[^cite_note-155] As early as 2003, however, some Uyghur groups wrote that their population was being vastly undercounted by Chinese authorities, claiming that their population actually exceeded 20 million while presenting "little evidence to support those figures".[^cite_note-gladney-156] Population disputes have continued into the present, with some activists and groups such as the [World Uyghur Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Uyghur_Congress) and [Uyghur American Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_American_Association) claiming that the Uyghur population ranges between 20 and 30 million.[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160] Some have even claimed that the real number of Uyghurs is actually 35 million.[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] Scholars, however, have generally rejected these claims, with Professor [Dru C. Gladney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dru_C._Gladney) writing in the 2004 book *[Xinjiang: China's Muslim Borderland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang:_China's_Muslim_Borderland)* that there is "scant evidence" to support Uyghur claims that their population within China exceeds 20 million.[^cite_note-SFStarr-163]

### Genetics

Genetic studies have suggested that the admixture between the [West Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basal_Eurasian) and [East Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_East_Asians) ancestral components of the Uyghurs may have been sex-biased, with East Asian ancestry being more common in maternal lineages and West Eurasian ancestry predominating in paternal lineages. Later genome-wide studies have indicated a more complex history involving multiple admixture events between East and West Eurasian populations.[^cite_note-Mol_Biol_Evol-2004-180][^cite_note-Genetics-2006-181]

A study of [mitochondrial DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_mitochondrial_DNA_haplogroup) (2004) (therefore the [matrilineal genetic contribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matrilineality)) found the frequency of Western Eurasian-specific [haplogroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup) in Uyghurs to be 42.6% and East Asian haplogroup to be 57.4%.[^cite_note-Mol_Biol_Evol-2004-180][^cite_note-Xu-2008-182] [Uyghurs in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghurs_in_Kazakhstan) on the other hand were shown to have 55% European/Western Eurasian maternal mtDNA.[^cite_note-Xu-2008-182]

A study based on [paternal DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y_haplogroup) (2005) shows West Eurasian haplogroups (J and R) in Uyghurs make up 65% to 70% and East Asian haplogroups (C, N, D and O) 30% to 35%.[^cite_note-Genetics-2006-181]

One study by Xu et al. (2008), using samples from Hetian ([Hotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotan)) only, found Uyghurs have about an average of 60% European or [West Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asia) (Western Eurasian) ancestry and about 40% [East Asian](19605700.md) or [Siberian](29639.md) ancestry (Eastern Eurasian). From the same area, it is found that the proportion of Uyghur individuals with European/West Asian ancestry ranges individually from 40.3% to 84.3% while their East Asian/Siberian ancestry ranges individually from 15.7% to 59.7%.[^cite_note-183] Further study by the same team showed an average of slightly greater European/West Asian component at 52% (ranging individually from 44.9% to 63.1%) in the Uyghur population in southern Xinjiang but only 47% (ranging individually from 30% to 55%) in the northern Uyghur population.[^cite_note-184]

&#x20;A different study by Li et al. (2009) used a larger sample of individuals from a wider area and found a higher East Asian component of about 70% on average, while the European/West Asian component was about 30%. Overall, Uyghur show relative more similarity to "Western East Asians" than to "Eastern East Asians". The authors also cite anthropologic studies which also estimate about 30% "Western proportions", which are in agreement with their genetic results.[^cite_note-cite_journal|pmc=2790568-185]



Genetic distance between different Eurasian populations and frequency of West- and East-Eurasian components.

[^cite_note-186]

A study (2013) based on [autosomal DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autosomal_DNA) shows that average Uyghurs are closest to other Turkic people in Central Asia and China as well as various Chinese populations. The analysis of the diversity of [cytochrome B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cytochrome_b) further suggests Uyghurs are closer to [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Chinese) and Siberian populations than to various [Caucasoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasoid) groups in West Asia or Europe. However, there is significant genetic distance between the Xinjiang's southern Uyghurs and Chinese population, but not between the northern Uyghurs and Chinese.[^cite_note-187]

A Study (2016) of Uyghur males living in southern Xinjiang used high-resolution 26 Y-STR loci system high-resolution to infer the genetic relationships between the Uyghur population and European and Asian populations. The results showed the Uyghur population of southern Xinjiang exhibited a genetic admixture of Eastern Asian and European populations but with slightly closer relationship with European populations than to Eastern Asian populations.[^cite_note-188]

An extensive genome study in 2017 analyzed 951 samples of Uyghurs from 14 geographical subpopulations in Xinjiang and observed a southwest and northeast differentiation in the population, partially caused by the [Tianshan Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tianshan_Mountains) which form a natural barrier, with gene flows from the east and west. The study identifies four major ancestral components that may have arisen from two earlier admixed groups: one that migrated from the west harboring a West-Eurasian component associated with European ancestry (25–37%) and a South Asian ancestry component (12–20%) and one from the east, harboring a Siberian ancestry component (15–17%) and an East Asian ancestry component (29–47%). In total, Uyghurs on average are 33.3% West Eurasian, 32.9% East Asian, 17.9% South Asian, and 16% Siberian. Western parts of Xinjiang are more West Eurasian components than East Eurasian. It suggests at least two major waves of admixture, one \~3,750 years ago coinciding with the age range of the mummies with European feature found in Xinjiang, and another occurring around 750 years ago.[^cite_note-189]

A 2018 study of 206 Uyghur samples from Xinjiang, using the ancestry-informative SNP (AISNP) analysis, found that the average genetic ancestry of Uyghurs is 63.7% East Asian-related and 36.3% European-related.[^cite_note-190]

## History

Main article: [History of the Uyghur people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Uyghur_people)



Uyghur princes from Cave 9 of the [Bezeklik Thousand Buddha Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bezeklik_Thousand_Buddha_Caves), Xinjiang, China, 8th–9th century AD, wall painting

The history of the Uyghur people, as with the ethnic origin of the people, is a matter of contention.[^cite_note-bovingdon-191] Uyghur historians viewed the Uyghurs as the original inhabitants of Xinjiang with a long history. Uyghur politician and historian Muhammad Amin Bughra wrote in his book *A History of East Turkestan*, stressing the Turkic aspects of his people, that the Turks have a continuous 9000-year-old history, while historian [Turghun Almas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turghun_Almas) incorporated discoveries of Tarim mummies to conclude that Uyghurs have over 6400 years of continuous history,[^cite_note-Tursun-192] and the [World Uyghur Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Uyghur_Congress) claimed a 4,000-year history in East Turkestan.[^cite_note-193] However, the official Chinese view, as documented in the white paper *History and Development of Xinjiang*, asserts that the Uyghur ethnic group formed after the collapse of the [Uyghur Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate) in 840, when the local residents of the Tarim Basin and its surrounding areas were merged with migrants from the khaganate.[^cite_note-194] The name "Uyghur" reappeared after the Soviet Union took the 9th-century ethnonym from the Uyghur Khaganate, then reapplied it to all non-nomadic Turkic Muslims of Xinjiang.[^cite_note-195] Many contemporary western scholars, however, do not consider the modern Uyghurs to be of direct linear descent from the old Uyghur Khaganate of Mongolia. Rather, they consider them to be descendants of a number of peoples, one of them the ancient Uyghurs.[^cite_note-xinjiang-107][^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillward2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA44_44\]-198]

### Early history

Discovery of well-preserved [Tarim mummies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarim_mummies) of a people European in appearance indicates the migration of a European-looking people into the Tarim area at the beginning of the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) around 1800 BC. These people may have been of [Tocharian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocharians) origin, and some have suggested them to be the [Yuezhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuezhi) mentioned in ancient Chinese texts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillward2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA14_14\]-199][^cite_note-200] The Tocharians are thought to have developed from the [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages) speaking [Afanasevo culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afanasevo_culture) of Southern Siberia (c. 3500–2500 BC).[^cite_note-201] A study published in 2021 showed that the earliest Tarim Basin cultures had high levels of [Ancient North Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_North_Eurasian) ancestry, with smaller admixture from [Northeast Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_East_Asians).[^cite_note-202] Later mummies overwhelmingly descended from the [Sintashta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sintashta_culture) and [Andronovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andronovo_culture) population, with smaller admixtures from [BMAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactria%E2%80%93Margiana_Archaeological_Complex) and the earlier Tarim mummies.[^cite_note-Zhang-2025-113] Uyghur activist Turgun Almas claimed that Tarim mummies were Uyghurs because the earliest Uyghurs practiced shamanism and the buried mummies' orientation suggests that they had been shamanists; meanwhile, Qurban Wäli claimed words written in Kharosthi and Sogdian scripts as "Uyghur" rather than Sogdian words absorbed into Uyghur according to other linguists.[^cite_note-203]

Later migrations brought peoples from the west and northwest to the Xinjiang region, probably speakers of various Iranian languages such as the [Saka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saka) tribes, who were closely related to the European [Scythians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythians) and descended from the earlier [Andronovo culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andronovo_culture),[^cite_note-204] and who may have been present in the [Khotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khotan) and [Kashgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgar) area in the first millennium BC, as well as the [Sogdians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sogdians) who formed networks of trading communities across the Tarim Basin from the 4th century AD.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillward2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA13_13\],_\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA29_29\]-205] There may also be an Indian component as the founding legend of [Khotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Khotan) suggests that the city was founded by Indians from [ancient Taxila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxila_\(ancient\)) during the reign of [Ashoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka).[^cite_note-Mallory_2000-206][^cite_note-207] Other people in the region mentioned in ancient Chinese texts include the [Dingling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dingling) as well as the [Xiongnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiongnu) who fought for supremacy in the region against the Chinese for several hundred years. Some Uyghur nationalists also claimed descent from the Xiongnu (according to the Chinese historical text the *[Book of Wei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Wei)*, the founder of the Uyghurs was descended from a Xiongnu ruler),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGolden1992155-94] but the view is contested by modern Chinese scholars.[^cite_note-Tursun-192]

The Yuezhi were driven away by the Xiongnu but founded the [Kushan Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kushan_Empire), which exerted some influence in the Tarim Basin, where [Kharosthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharosthi) texts have been found in [Loulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loulan_Kingdom), [Niya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niya_\(Tarim_Basin\)) and [Khotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Khotan). Loulan and Khotan were some of the many city-states that existed in the Xinjiang region during the [Han dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_dynasty); others include [Kucha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kucha), [Turfan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turfan), [Karasahr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karasahr) and [Kashgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgar). These kingdoms in the Tarim Basin came under the control of China during the Han and Tang dynasties. During the [Tang dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_dynasty) they were conquered and placed under the control of the [Protectorate General to Pacify the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectorate_General_to_Pacify_the_West), and the Indo-European cultures of these kingdoms never recovered from Tang rule after thousands of their inhabitants were killed during the conquest.[^cite_note-208] The settled population of these cities later merged with the incoming Turkic people, including the Uyghurs of Uyghur Khaganate, to form the modern Uyghurs. The Indo-European [Tocharian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocharian_language) later disappeared as the urban population switched to a Turkic language such as the [Old Uyghur language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Uyghur_language).[^cite_note-209]

The early [Turkic peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) descended from agricultural communities in [Northeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Asia) who moved westwards into [Mongolia](19271.md) in the late 3rd millennium BC, where they adopted a pastoral lifestyle.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobbeets2017216–218-210][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobbeets2020-211][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENelson_et_al.2020-212][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi_et_al.2020-213][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUchiyama_et_al.2020-214] By the early 1st millennium BC, these peoples had become [equestrian nomads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equestrian_nomads).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobbeets2017216–218-210] In subsequent centuries, the steppe populations of [Central Asia](6742.md) appear to have been progressively [Turkified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification) by [East Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian_people) nomadic Turks, moving out of Mongolia.[^cite_note-Damgaard_Conclusion-215][^cite_note-Kuang_Lee_197-216]

### Uyghur Khaganate (8th–9th centuries)

Main articles: [Uyghur Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate) and [Toquz Oghuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toquz_Oghuz)



[Bögü Qaghan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%B6g%C3%BC_Qaghan), the third Khagan of the [Uyghur Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate), in a suit of armor; 8th century [Manichean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manichean) manuscript ([MIK III 4979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leaf_from_a_Manichaean_book_MIK_III_4979))

The Uyghurs of the Uyghur Khaganate were part of a Turkic confederation called the [Tiele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiele_people),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGolden1992157-217] who lived in the valleys south of [Lake Baikal](56865.md) and around the [Yenisei River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yenisei_River). They overthrew the [First Turkic Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Turkic_Khaganate) and established the [Uyghur Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate).

The Uyghur Khaganate lasted from 744 to 840.[^cite_note-xinjiang-107] It was administered from the imperial capital [Ordu-Baliq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordu-Baliq), one of the biggest ancient cities built in Mongolia. In 840, following a famine and civil war, the Uyghur Khaganate was overrun by the [Yenisei Kirghiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yenisei_Kirghiz), another Turkic people. As a result, the majority of tribal groups formerly under Uyghur control dispersed and moved out of Mongolia.

### Uyghur kingdoms (9th–11th centuries)



Uyghur Khaganate in geopolitical context c. 820 AD

The Uyghurs who founded the Uyghur Khaganate dispersed after the fall of the Khaganate, to live among the [Karluks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karluks) and to places such as [Jimsar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimsar_County), [Turpan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turpan) and [Gansu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gansu).[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-220] These Uyghurs soon founded two kingdoms and the easternmost state was the [Ganzhou Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganzhou_Uyghur_Kingdom) (870–1036) which ruled parts of Xinjiang, with its capital near present-day [Zhangye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhangye), Gansu, China. The modern [Yugurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugur) are believed to be descendants of these Uyghurs. Ganzhou was absorbed by the [Western Xia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Xia) in 1036.

The second Uyghur kingdom, the [Kingdom of Qocho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Qocho) ruled a larger section of Xinjiang, also known as *Uyghuristan* in its later period, was founded in the Turpan area with its capital in Qocho (modern [Gaochang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaochang)) and [Beshbalik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimsar_County). The Kingdom of Qocho lasted from the ninth to the fourteenth century and proved to be longer-lasting than any power in the region, before or since.[^cite_note-xinjiang-107] The Uyghurs were originally [Tengrists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tengrism), shamanists, and [Manichaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manichaeism), but converted to Buddhism during this period. Qocho accepted the [Qara Khitai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qara_Khitai) as its overlord in the 1130s, and in 1209 submitted voluntarily to the rising [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire). The Uyghurs of Kingdom of Qocho were allowed significant autonomy and played an important role as civil servants to the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire), but was finally destroyed by the [Chagatai Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_Khanate) by the end of the 14th century.[^cite_note-xinjiang-107][^cite_note-221]

### Islamization

Main article: [Turkic settlement of the Tarim Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_settlement_of_the_Tarim_Basin)

In the tenth century, the [Karluks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karluks), [Yagmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yagma), [Chigils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chigils) and other Turkic tribes founded the [Kara-Khanid Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara-Khanid_Khanate) in [Semirechye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jetisu), Western [Tian Shan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tian_Shan), and [Kashgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgaria) and later conquered [Transoxiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transoxiana). The Karakhanid rulers were likely to be Yaghmas who were associated with the [Toquz Oghuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toquz_Oghuz) and some historians therefore see this as a link between the Karakhanid and the Uyghurs of the Uyghur Khaganate, although this connection is disputed by others.[^cite_note-Millward2007-222]

The Karakhanids converted to Islam in the tenth century beginning with [Sultan Satuq Bughra Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_Satuq_Bughra_Khan), the first Turkic dynasty to do so.[^cite_note-sinor-223] Modern Uyghurs see the Muslim Karakhanids as an important part of their history; however, Islamization of the people of the Tarim Basin was a gradual process. The Indo-Iranian [Saka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakas) Buddhist [Kingdom of Khotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Khotan) was conquered by the Turkic Muslim Karakhanids from Kashgar in the early 11th century, but Uyghur Qocho remained mainly Buddhist until the 15th century, and the conversion of the Uyghur people to Islam was not completed until the 17th century.



[Chagatai Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_Khanate) ([Moghulistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moghulistan)) in 1490

The 12th and 13th century saw the domination by non-Muslim powers: first the [Kara-Khitans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara-Khitans) in the 12th century, followed by the [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire) in the 13th century. After the death of [Genghis Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genghis_Khan) in 1227, Transoxiana and Kashgar became the domain of his second son, [Chagatai Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_Khan). The [Chagatai Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_Khanate) split into two in the 1340s, and the area of the Chagatai Khanate where the modern Uyghurs live became part of [Moghulistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moghulistan), which meant "land of the Mongols". In the 14th century, a Chagatayid khan [Tughluq Temür](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tughlugh_Timur) converted to Islam, Genghisid [Mongol nobilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongolian_nobility) also followed him to convert to Islam. His son [Khizr Khoja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khizr_Khoja) conquered Qocho and Turfan (the core of Uyghuristan) in the 1390s, and the Uyghurs there became largely Muslim by the beginning of the 16th century.[^cite_note-Millward2007-222] After being converted to Islam, the descendants of the previously [Buddhist Uyghurs in Turfan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Qocho) failed to retain memory of their ancestral legacy and falsely believed that the "infidel Kalmuks" ([Dzungars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungar_people)) were the ones who built Buddhist structures in their area.[^cite_note-GibbLewis1998-224]

From the late 14th through 17th centuries, the Xinjiang region became further subdivided into Moghulistan in the north, [Altishahr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altishahr) (Kashgar and the Tarim Basin), and the Turfan area, each often ruled separately by competing Chagatayid descendants, the [Dughlats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dughlats), and later the [Khojas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoja_\(Turkestan\)).[^cite_note-Millward2007-222]

Islam was also spread by the [Sufis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufis), and branches of its [Naqshbandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqshbandi) order were the [Khojas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoja_\(Turkestan\)) who seized control of political and military affairs in the Tarim Basin and Turfan in the 17th century. The Khojas however split into two rival factions, the *Aqtaghlik* ("White Mountainers") Khojas (also called the [Afaqiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afaq_Khoja)) and the *Qarataghlik* ("Black Mountainers") Khojas (also called the Ishaqiyya). The legacy of the Khojas lasted until the 19th century. The Qarataghlik Khojas seized power in Yarkand where the Chagatai Khans ruled in the Yarkent Khanate, forcing the Aqtaghlik Afaqi Khoja into exile.

### Qing rule



Uyghur General [Khojis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khojis) (−1781), governor of [Us-Turfan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Us-Turfan), who later resided at the Qing court in Beijing. Painting by a European Jesuit artist at the Chinese court in 1775, collection in [Ziguangge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziguangge), [Zhongnanhai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhongnanhai), [Beijing](18603746.md)

[^cite_note-225]

In the 17th century, the Buddhist [Dzungar Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungar_Khanate) grew in power in [Dzungaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungaria). The [Dzungar conquest of Altishahr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungar_conquest_of_Altishahr) ended the last independent Chagatai Khanate, the [Yarkent Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarkent_Khanate), after the Aqtaghlik [Afaq Khoja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afaq_Khoja) sought aid from the [5th Dalai Lama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5th_Dalai_Lama) and his Dzungar Buddhist followers to help him in his struggle against the Qarataghlik Khojas. The Aqtaghlik Khojas in the Tarim Basin then became vassals to the Dzungars.

The expansion of the Dzungars into [Khalkha Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalkha_Mongols) territory in [Mongolia](19271.md) brought them into direct conflict with [Qing China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty) in the late 17th century, and in the process also brought Chinese presence back into the region a thousand years after Tang China lost control of the [Western Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Regions).[^cite_note-226]



Minaret of [Turpan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turpan) ruler [Emin Khoja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emin_Khoja), built by his son and successor Suleiman in 1777 in the memory of his father (tallest minaret in China)

The [Dzungar–Qing War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungar%E2%80%93Qing_War) lasted a decade. During the Dzungar conflict, two Aqtaghlik brothers, the so-called "Younger Khoja" ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language): 霍集佔), also known as Khwāja-i Jahān, and his sibling, the Elder Khoja ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language): 波羅尼都), also known as Burhān al-Dīn, after being appointed as vassals in the Tarim Basin by the Dzungars, first joined the Qing and rebelled against Dzungar rule until the final Qing victory over the Dzungars, then they rebelled against the Qing in the [Revolt of the Altishahr Khojas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolt_of_the_Altishahr_Khojas) (1757–1759), an action which prompted the invasion and conquest of the Tarim Basin by the Qing in 1759. The Uyghurs of Turfan and Hami such as [Emin Khoja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emin_Khoja) were allies of the Qing in this conflict, and these Uyghurs also helped the Qing rule the Altishahr Uyghurs in the Tarim Basin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillward2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA101_101\]-227][^cite_note-228]

The [final campaign against the Dzungars in the 1750s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ten_Great_Campaigns) ended with the [Dzungar genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungar_genocide). The Qing "final solution" of genocide to solve the problem of the Dzungar Mongols created a land devoid of Dzungars, which was followed by the Qing sponsored settlement of millions of other people in Dzungaria.[^cite_note-Zungar2-229][^cite_note-230] In northern Xinjiang, the Qing brought in Han, Hui, Uyghur, Xibe, Daurs, Solons, Turkic Muslim Taranchis and Kazakh colonists, with one third of Xinjiang's total population consisting of Hui and Han in the northern area, while around two thirds were Uyghurs in southern Xinjiang's Tarim Basin.[^cite_note-ed._Starr_2004-231] In Dzungaria, the Qing established new cities like Ürümqi and Yining.[^cite_note-232] The [Dzungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungaria) basin itself is now inhabited by many Kazakhs.[^cite_note-233] The Qing therefore unified Xinjiang and changed its demographic composition as well.[^cite_note-Liu_&_Faure_1996-234]<sup>: 71 </sup> The crushing of the Buddhist Dzungars by the Qing led to the empowerment of the Muslim Begs in southern Xinjiang, migration of Muslim Taranchis to northern Xinjiang, and increasing Turkic Muslim power, with Turkic Muslim culture and identity was tolerated or even promoted by the Qing.[^cite_note-Liu_&_Faure_1996-234]<sup>: 76 </sup> It was therefore argued by Henry Schwarz that "the Qing victory was, in a certain sense, a victory for Islam".[^cite_note-Liu_&_Faure_1996-234]<sup>: 72 </sup>

In [Beijing](18603746.md), a community of Uyghurs was clustered around the mosque near the [Forbidden City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbidden_City), having moved to Beijing in the 18th century.[^cite_note-235]

The [Ush rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ush_rebellion?action=edit\&redlink=1) in 1765 by Uyghurs against the [Manchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchus) occurred after several incidents of misrule and abuse that had caused considerable anger and resentment.[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillward2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA108_108\]-238] The Manchu Emperor ordered that the Uyghur rebel town be massacred, and the men were executed and the women and children enslaved.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillward2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA109_109\]-239]

### Yettishar

During the [Dungan Revolt (1862–1877)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dungan_Revolt_\(1862%E2%80%931877\)), Andijani Uzbeks from the [Khanate of Kokand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate_of_Kokand) under [Buzurg Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buzurg_Khan?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [Yaqub Beg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakub_Beg_of_Yettishar) expelled Qing officials from parts of southern Xinjiang and founded an independent Kashgarian kingdom called [Yettishar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yettishar) ("Country of Seven Cities"). Under the leadership of Yaqub Beg, it included [Kashgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgar), [Yarkand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarkant_County), [Khotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotan), [Aksu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aksu,_Xinjiang), [Kucha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kucha), [Korla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korla), and [Turpan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turpan). Large Qing dynasty forces under Chinese General [Zuo Zongtang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zuo_Zongtang) attacked Yettishar in 1876.

### Qing reconquest

After this invasion, the two regions of Dzungaria, which had been known as the Dzungar region or the Northern marches of the Tian Shan,[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] and the Tarim Basin, which had been known as "Muslim land" or southern marches of the Tian Shan,[^cite_note-246] were reorganized into a province named *Xinjiang*, meaning "New Territory".[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248]

### First East Turkestan Republic

In 1912, the Qing dynasty was replaced by the Republic of China. By 1920, Pan-Turkic [Jadidists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jadidism) had become a challenge to Chinese warlord Yang Zengxin, who controlled Xinjiang. Uyghurs staged several uprisings against Chinese rule. In 1931, the [Kumul Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumul_Rebellion) erupted, leading to the establishment of an independent government in Khotan in 1932,[^cite_note-249] which later led to the creation of the [First East Turkestan Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_East_Turkestan_Republic), officially known as the Turkish Islamic Republic of East Turkestan. Uyghurs joined with Uzbeks, Kazakhs, and Kyrgyz and successfully declared their independence on 12 November 1933.[^cite_note-250] The First East Turkestan Republic was a short-lived attempt at independence around the areas encompassing Kashgar, Yarkent, and Khotan, and it was attacked during the Qumul Rebellion by a [Chinese Muslim army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/36th_Division_\(National_Revolutionary_Army\)) under [General Ma Zhancang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Zhancang) and [Ma Fuyuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Fuyuan) and fell following the Battle of Kashgar (1934). The Soviets backed Chinese warlord Sheng Shicai's rule over East Turkestan/Xinjiang from 1934 to 1943. In April 1937, remnants of the First East Turkestan Republic launched an uprising known as the [Islamic Rebellion in Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Rebellion_in_Xinjiang_\(1937\)) and briefly established an independent government, controlling areas from Atush, Kashgar, Yarkent, and even parts of Khotan, before it was crushed in October 1937, following Soviet intervention.[^cite_note-Starr-2015-251] Sheng Shicai purged 50,000 to 100,000 people, mostly Uyghurs, following this uprising.[^cite_note-Starr-2015-251]

### Second East Turkestan Republic

The oppressive reign of [Sheng Shicai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheng_Shicai) fueled discontent by Uyghur and other Turkic peoples of the region, and Sheng expelled Soviet advisors following U.S. support for the [Kuomintang](16903.md) of the [Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_\(1912%E2%80%931949\)).[^cite_note-252] This led the Soviets to capitalize on the Uyghur and other Turkic people's discontent in the region, culminating in their support of the [Ili Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ili_Rebellion) in October 1944. The Ili Rebellion resulted in the establishment of the [Second East Turkestan Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_East_Turkestan_Republic) on 12 November 1944, in the three districts of what is now the [Ili Kazakh Autonomous Prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ili_Kazakh_Autonomous_Prefecture).[^cite_note-253] Several pro-KMT Uyghurs like [Isa Yusuf Alptekin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isa_Yusuf_Alptekin), Memet Emin Bugra, and Mesut Sabri opposed the [Second East Turkestan Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_East_Turkestan_Republic) and supported the Republic of China.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-Klimeš2015_2-255][^cite_note-256] In the summer of 1949, the Soviets purged the thirty top leaders of the Second East Turkestan Republic[^cite_note-257] and its five top officials died in a mysterious plane crash on 27 August 1949.[^cite_note-258] On 13 October 1949, the People's Liberation Army entered the region and the East Turkestan [National Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ili_National_Army) was merged into the PLA's 5th Army Corps, leading to the official end of the Second East Turkestan Republic on 22 December 1949.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261]

### Contemporary era
