Ethnolinguistic map of Xinjiang in 1967



Map showing the distribution of ethnicities in [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) according to census figures from 2000. The prefectures with Uyghur majorities are in blue.

Mao declared the founding of the [People's Republic of China](5405.md) on 1 October 1949. He turned the Second East Turkistan Republic into the [Ili Kazakh Autonomous Prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ili_Kazakh_Autonomous_Prefecture), and appointed [Saifuddin Azizi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saifuddin_Azizi) as the region's first Communist Party governor. Many Republican loyalists fled into exile in Turkey and Western countries. The name Xinjiang was changed to Xinjiang Uyghur Autonomous Region, where Uyghurs are the largest ethnicity, mostly concentrated in the south-western Xinjiang.[^cite_note-263]

The [Xinjiang conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_conflict) is a separatist conflict in China's far-west province of Xinjiang, whose northern region is known as [Dzungaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungaria) and whose southern region (the [Tarim Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarim_Basin)) is known as East Turkestan. Uyghur separatists and independence groups claim that the Second East Turkestan Republic was illegally incorporated by China in 1949 and has since been under Chinese occupation. Uyghur identity remains fragmented, as some support a [Pan-Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Islamism) vision, exemplified by the [East Turkestan Islamic Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Turkestan_Islamic_Movement), while others support a [Pan-Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Turkism) vision, such as the [East Turkestan Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Turkestan_Liberation_Organization). A third group which includes the [Uyghur American Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_American_Association) supports a [western liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_liberal_democracies) vision and hopes for a US-led intervention into Xinjiang.[^cite_note-Christofferson-2002-264] Some Uyghur fighters in Syria have also studied [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) as a model for their homeland.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266] As a result, "no Uyghur or East Turkestan group speaks for all Uyghurs", and Uyghurs in Pan-Turkic and Pan-Islamic camps have committed violence including assassinations on other Uyghurs who they think are too assimilated to Chinese society.[^cite_note-Christofferson-2002-264]

Uyghur activists like [Rebiya Kadeer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebiya_Kadeer) have mainly tried to garner international support for Uyghurs, including the right to demonstrate, although China's government has accused her of orchestrating the deadly [July 2009 Ürümqi riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2009_%C3%9Cr%C3%BCmqi_riots).[^cite_note-267] Eric Enno Tamm's 2011 book stated that "authorities have censored Uyghur writers and 'lavished funds' on official histories that depict Chinese territorial expansion into ethnic borderlands as 'unifications (tongyi), never as conquests (zhengfu) or [annexations](18950961.md) (tunbing)' "[^cite_note-268]

#### Human rights abuses against Uyghurs in Xinjiang

Main articles: [Persecution of Uyghurs in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Uyghurs_in_China) and [Xinjiang internment camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_internment_camps)

In 2014, the Chinese government announced a "[people's war on terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_war_on_terror)". Since then, Uyghurs in Xinjiang have been affected by extensive controls and restrictions which the Chinese Communist Party (CCP) and the Chinese government has imposed upon their religious, cultural, economic and social lives.[^cite_note-Dou-2022-269][^cite_note-aj2018-270][^cite_note-271][^cite_note-hrw\._UN\:U-272] In order to [forcibly assimilate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_assimilation) them, the government has [arbitrarily detained](13435705.md) more than an estimated one million Uyghurs in [internment camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_internment_camps).[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) says that the camps have been used to indoctrinate Uyghurs and other Muslims since 2017.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276]

Leaked Chinese government operating procedures state that the main feature of the camps is to ensure adherence to [CCP ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), with the inmates being continuously held captive in the camps for a minimum of 12 months depending on their performance on Chinese ideology tests.[^cite_note-277] *[The New York Times](30680.md)* has reported inmates are required to "sing hymns praising the Chinese Communist Party and write 'self-criticism' essays," and that prisoners are also subjected to physical and verbal abuse by prison guards.[^cite_note-nyt-detention-278] Chinese officials have sometimes been assigned to monitor the families of current inmates, and women have been detained due to actions by their sons or husbands.[^cite_note-nyt-detention-278]

&#x20;Other policies have included [forced labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labor),[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] suppression of Uyghur [religious practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_China),[^cite_note-281] political [indoctrination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indoctrination),[^cite_note-reut_Musl-282] severe ill-treatment,[^cite_note-bhrc-283] [forced sterilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_sterilization),[^cite_note-apne_Chin-284] forced [contraception](18978770.md),[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] and [forced abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_abortion).[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] According to German researcher [Adrian Zenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrian_Zenz), hundreds of thousands of children have been forcibly separated from their parents and sent to [boarding schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boarding_schools_in_China).[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290] The [Australian Strategic Policy Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Strategic_Policy_Institute) estimates that some sixteen thousand [mosques](19894.md) have been razed or damaged since 2017.[^cite_note-291] [Associated Press](18935732.md) reported that from 2015 to 2018, [birth rates](515898.md) in the mostly Uyghur regions of [Hotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotan) and [Kashgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgar) fell by more than 60%,[^cite_note-apne_Chin-284] compared to a decrease by 9.69% in the whole country.[^cite_note-292] The allegation of Uyghur birth rates being lower than those of Han Chinese have been disputed by pundits from *[Pakistan Observer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Observer)*,[^cite_note-293] [Antara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antara_\(news_agency\)),[^cite_note-294] and [Detik.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detik.com).[^cite_note-295]&#x20;



Protesters in Amsterdam with the [Flag of East Turkestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_East_Turkestan)

The policies have drawn widespread condemnation, with some characterizing them as a genocide. In an [assessment by the UN Human Rights Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Xinjiang_Report), the [United Nations](31769.md) (UN) stated that China's policies and actions in the Xinjiang region may be [crimes against humanity](61099.md), although it did not use the term genocide.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297] The United States[^cite_note-298] and legislatures in several countries have described the policies as a genocide. The Chinese government denies having committed human rights abuses in Xinjiang[^cite_note-Finley-2020-299][^cite_note-300] and claims that these camps served the goals of promoting CCP ideology, preventing [separatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Turkestan_independence_movement), fighting [terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_conflict), and providing [vocational education](56551.md) to Uyghurs.[^cite_note-301]

## Uyghurs of Taoyuan, Hunan

Around 5,000 Uyghurs live around [Taoyuan County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoyuan_County) and other parts of [Changde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changde) in [Hunan](166409.md) province.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] They are descended from [Hala Bashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hala_Bashi), a Uyghur leader from [Turpan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turpan) ([Kingdom of Qocho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Qocho)), and his Uyghur soldiers sent to Hunan by the Ming Emperor in the 14th century to crush the Miao rebels during the [Miao Rebellions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miao_Rebellions_\(Ming_Dynasty\)) in the Ming dynasty.[^cite_note-english.people.com.cn-39][^cite_note-304] The 1982 census recorded 4,000 Uyghurs in Hunan.[^cite_note-305] They have genealogies which survive 600 years later to the present day. Genealogy keeping is a Han Chinese custom which the Hunan Uyghurs adopted. These Uyghurs were given the surname Jian by the Emperor.[^cite_note-306] There is some confusion as to whether they practice Islam or not. Some say that they have assimilated with the Han and do not practice Islam anymore and only their genealogies indicate their Uyghur ancestry.[^cite_note-307] Chinese news sources report that they are Muslim.[^cite_note-english.people.com.cn-39]

The Uyghur troops led by Hala were ordered by the Ming Emperor to crush [Miao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miao_people) rebellions and were given titles by him. Jian is the predominant surname among the Uyghur in Changde, Hunan. Another group of Uyghur have the surname Sai. [Hui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people) and Uyghur have intermarried in the Hunan area. The Hui are descendants of Arabs and Han Chinese who intermarried and they share the Islamic religion with the Uyghur in Hunan. It is reported that they now number around 10,000 people. The Uyghurs in Changde are not very religious and eat pork. Older Uyghurs disapprove of this, especially elders at the mosques in Changde and they seek to draw them back to Islamic customs.[^cite_note-Shih-308]

In addition to eating pork, the Uyghurs of [Changde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changde) Hunan practice other Han Chinese customs, like ancestor worship at graves. Some Uyghurs from Xinjiang visit the Hunan Uyghurs out of curiosity or interest. Also, the Uyghurs of Hunan do not speak the [Uyghur language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_language), instead, they speak Mandarin as their native language and Arabic for religious reasons at the mosque.[^cite_note-Shih-308]

## Culture

### Religion



A [Uyghur mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khotan_Mosque) in [Khotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotan)

The ancient Uyghurs believed in many local deities. These practices gave rise to [shamanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamanism) and [Tengrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tengrism). Uyghurs also practiced aspects of [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism) such as [fire altars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_altar), and adopted [Manichaeism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manichaeism) as a state religion for the Uyghur Khaganate,[^cite_note-309] possibly in 762 or 763. Ancient Uyghurs also practiced [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Central_Asia) after they moved to Qocho, and some believed in [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East).[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313]

People in the Western Tarim Basin region began their conversion to Islam early in the Kara-Khanid Khanate period.[^cite_note-sinor-223] Some pre-Islamic practices continued under Muslim rule; for example, while the Quran dictated many rules on marriage and divorce, other pre-Islamic principles based on Zoroastrianism also helped shape the laws of the land.[^cite_note-314] There had been [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Xinjiang) conversions in the late 19th and early 20th centuries, but these were suppressed by the [First East Turkestan Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_East_Turkestan_Republic) government agents.[^cite_note-UhalleyWu2015-315][^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317] Because of persecution, the churches were destroyed and the believers were scattered.[^cite_note-Millward27-318] According to the national census, 0.5% or 1,142 [Uyghurs in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghurs_in_Kazakhstan) were [Christians](18337522.md) in 2009.[^cite_note-319]

Modern Uyghurs are primarily [Muslim](19541.md) and they are the second-largest predominantly Muslim ethnicity in China after the [Hui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people).[^cite_note-Palmer-320] The majority of modern Uyghurs are [Sunnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam), although additional conflicts exist between Sufi and non-Sufi religious orders.[^cite_note-Palmer-320] While modern Uyghurs consider Islam to be part of their identity, religious observance varies between different regions. In general, Muslims in the southern region, Kashgar in particular, are more conservative. For example, women wearing the veil (a piece of cloth covering the head completely) are more common in Kashgar than some other cities.[^cite_note-321] The veil, however, has been banned in cities such as [Ürümqi](199155.md) since 2014 after it became more popular there.[^cite_note-322]

While the vast majority of Uyghur Muslims had been Sunni, there had been some Shia Uyghurs, mostly Ismaili with a Twelver minority. Ismaili dawah networks were known to have reached Uyghur regions in Xinjiang via Badakhshan and the Pamirs, where pockets of Nizari and Tayyibi Shia Islam were already present among Iranic and Turkic mountain communities.[^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324] A minority of Uyghur Shias were Twelver.[^cite_note-325] In the early 20th century, some Uyghurs had studied in Twelver Shia seminaries in Najaf and Qom and later returned to Xinjiang as Twelver Shia Muslims.[^cite_note-Millward-1-326] During the Republican and Maoist eras in China, Shia Muslims were marginalized both nationally by the Han Chinese authorities, and regionally by Sunni Uyghur religious elites.[^cite_note-Millward-1-326] Twelver Shia Islam was historically associated with Persians or Tajiks, limiting its adoption among Uyghurs. Nevertheless, many Uyghurs historically converted to Shia Islam.[^cite_note-327]

There is an ethnic split between the Uyghurs and the Hui Muslims in Xinjiang and they generally worship in different mosques.[^cite_note-328] The Chinese government discourages religious worship among the Uyghurs,[^cite_note-nyt2016-329] and there is evidence of thousands of Uyghur mosques including historic ones being destroyed.[^cite_note-lily-330] According to a 2020 [Australian Strategic Policy Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Strategic_Policy_Institute) report, Chinese authorities since 2017 have destroyed or damaged 16,000 mosques in Xinjiang.[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332]

In the early 21st century, a new trend of Islam, [Salafism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_movement), emerged in Xinjiang, mostly among the Turkic population including Uyghurs, although there are Hui Salafis. These Salafis tended to demonstrate [pan-Islamism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Islamism) and abandoned nationalism in favor of a caliphate to rule Xinjiang in the event of independence from China.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334] Many Uyghur Salafis have allied themselves with the [Turkistan Islamic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkistan_Islamic_Party) in response to growing repression of Uyghurs by China.[^cite_note-335]

### Language

Main article: [Uyghur language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_language)



Map of language families in Xinjiang

The ancient people of the Tarim Basin originally spoke different languages, such as [Tocharian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocharian_languages), [Saka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saka_language) (Khotanese), and [Gandhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C4%81ndh%C4%81r%C4%AB_language). The Turkic people who moved into the region in the 9th century brought with them their languages, which slowly supplanted the original tongues of the local inhabitants. In the 11th century, [Mahmud al-Kashgari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_al-Kashgari) noted that the Uyghurs (of Qocho) spoke a pure Turkic language, but they also still spoke another language among themselves and had two different scripts. He also noted that the people of Khotan did not know Turkic well and had their own language and script ([Khotanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saka_language)).[^cite_note-336] Writers of the Karakhanid period, Al-Kashgari and [Yusuf Balasagun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_Balasagun), referred to their Turkic language as *Khāqāniyya* (meaning royal) or the "language of Kashgar" or simply Turkic.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338]

The modern Uyghur language is classified under the [Karluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karluk_languages) branch of the [Turkic language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages). It is closely related to [Äynu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%84ynu_language), [Lop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lop_language), [Ili Turki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ili_Turki_language) and [Chagatay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatay_language) (the East Karluk languages) and slightly less closely to [Uzbek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbek_language) (which is West Karluk). The Uyghur language is an [agglutinative language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agglutinative_language) and has a [subject-object-verb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subject-object-verb) word order. It has [vowel harmony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vowel_harmony) like other Turkic languages and has noun and verb [cases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammatical_case) but lacks distinction of gender forms.[^cite_note-339]

Modern Uyghurs have adopted a number of scripts for their language. The [Arabic script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_alphabet), known as the [Chagatay alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatay_alphabet), was adopted along with Islam. This alphabet is known as Kona Yëziq (old script). Political changes in the 20th century led to numerous reforms of the scripts, for example the [Cyrillic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrillic_script)-based [Uyghur Cyrillic alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Cyrillic_alphabet), a Latin [Uyghur New Script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_New_Script) and later a reformed [Uyghur Arabic alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Arabic_alphabet), which represents all vowels, unlike Kona Yëziq. A new Latin version, the [Uyghur Latin alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Latin_alphabet), was also devised in the 21st century.

In the 1990s, many Uyghurs in parts of Xinjiang could not speak [Mandarin Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese).[^cite_note-340]

### Literature

Main article: [Uyghur literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_literature)



Leaf from a [Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Khaganate)-[Manichaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Manichaeism) version of the *[Arzhang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arzhang)*

The literary works of the ancient Uyghurs were mostly translations of Buddhist and Manichaean religious texts,[^cite_note-341] but there were also narrative, poetic and epic works apparently original to the Uyghurs. However it is the literature of the Kara-Khanid period that is considered by modern Uyghurs to be the important part of their literary traditions. Among these are Islamic religious texts and histories of [Turkic peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples), and important works surviving from that era are *[Kutadgu Bilig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutadgu_Bilig)*, "Wisdom of Royal Glory" by [Yusuf Khass Hajib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_Khass_Hajib) (1069–70), [Mahmud al-Kashgari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_al-Kashgari)'s *Dīwānu l-Luġat al-Turk*, "A Dictionary of Turkic Dialects" (1072) and Ehmed Yükneki's *Etebetulheqayiq*. Modern Uyghur religious literature includes the [Taẕkirah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tadhkirah_\(Ahmadiyya\)), biographies of Islamic religious figures and saints.[^cite_note-342][^cite_note-Thum2012-125][^cite_note-343] The Turki language *Tadhkirah i Khwajagan* was written by M. Sadiq Kashghari.[^cite_note-Storey2002-344] Between the 1600s and 1900s many Turki-language tazkirah manuscripts devoted to stories of local sultans, martyrs and saints were written.[^cite_note-345] Perhaps the most famous and best-loved pieces of modern Uyghur literature are [Abdurehim Ötkür](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdurehim_%C3%96tk%C3%BCr)'s *Iz*, *Oyghanghan Zimin*, [Zordun Sabir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zordun_Sabir)'s *Anayurt* and [Ziya Samedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziya_Samedi)'s novels *Mayimkhan* and *Mystery of the years*.

Exiled Uyghur writers and poets, such as [Muyesser Abdul'ehed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muyesser_Abdul'ehed), use literature to highlight the issues facing their community.[^cite_note-346]

### Music



Uyghur Meshrep musicians in Yarkand

[Muqam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muqam) is the classical musical style. The **12 Muqams** are the national oral epic of the Uyghurs. The muqam system was developed among the Uyghur in northwestern China and Central Asia over approximately the last 1500 years from the Arabic [maqamat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqama) [modal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mode_\(music\)) system that has led to many musical genres among peoples of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) and [North Africa](21714.md). Uyghurs have local muqam systems named after the oasis towns of [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang), such as [Dolan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolan_people), [Ili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yining), [Kumul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hami) and [Turpan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turpan). The most fully developed at this point is the Western [Tarim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarim_Basin) region's 12 muqams, which are now a large canon of music and songs recorded by the traditional performers [Turdi Akhun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turdi_Akhun) and [Omar Akhun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Akhun) among others in the 1950s and edited into a more systematic system. Although the folk performers probably improvized their songs, as in Turkish [taksim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taqsim) performances, the present institutional canon is performed as fixed compositions by ensembles.

The Uyghur Muqam of Xinjiang has been designated by [UNESCO](21786641.md) as part of the Intangible Heritage of Humanity.[^cite_note-347]

[Amannisa Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amannisa_Khan), sometimes called Amanni Shahan (1526–1560), is credited with collecting and thereby preserving the Twelve Muqam.[^cite_note-348] Russian scholar Pantusov writes that the Uyghurs manufactured their own musical instruments, they had 62 different kinds of musical instruments, and in every Uyghur home there used to be an instrument called a "[duttar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutar)".

Uzbek composer [Shakhida Shaimardanova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shakhida_Shaimardanova) uses themes from Uyghur folk music in her compositions.[^cite_note-349]

### Dance

[Sanam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanam_\(dance\)) is a popular folk dance among the Uyghur people.[^cite_note-350] It is commonly danced by people at weddings, festive occasions, and parties.[^cite_note-abliz-351] The dance may be performed with singing and musical accompaniment. Sama is a form of group dance for [Newruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newruz) (New Year) and other festivals.[^cite_note-abliz-351] Other dances include the Dolan dances, Shadiyane, and Nazirkom.[^cite_note-352] Some dances may alternate between singing and dancing, and Uyghur hand-drums called *[dap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dap_\(drum\))* are commonly used as accompaniment for Uyghur dances.

### Art



Wall painting at [Bezeklik caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bezeklik_Thousand_Buddha_Caves) in Flaming Mountains, Turpan Depression



Xinjiang carpet factory

During the late-19th and early-20th centuries, scientific and archaeological expeditions to the region of Xinjiang's [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road) discovered numerous cave temples, monastery ruins, and wall paintings, as well as miniatures, books, and documents. There are 77 [rock-cut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_cut_architecture) caves at the site. Most have rectangular spaces with [round arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Round_arch) ceilings often divided into four sections, each with a [mural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mural) of [Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha). The effect is of an entire ceiling covered with hundreds of Buddha murals. Some ceilings are painted with a large Buddha surrounded by other figures, including Indians, [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persians) and Europeans. The quality of the murals vary with some being artistically naïve while others are masterpieces of religious art.[^cite_note-353]

### Education

Historically, the education level of Old Uyghur people was higher than the other ethnicities around them. The Buddhist Uyghurs of Qocho became the civil servants of Mongol Empire and Old Uyghur Buddhists enjoyed a high status in the Mongol empire. They also introduced the written [script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongolian_script) for the [Mongolian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongolian_language). In the Islamic era, education was provided by the mosques and [madrassas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrasa). During the Qing era, Chinese Confucian schools were also set up in Xinjiang[^cite_note-Millward_PA142-354] and in the late 19th century Christian missionary schools.[^cite_note-benson-355]

In the late nineteenth and early 20th century, schools were often located in mosques and madrassas. Mosques ran informal schools, known as [mektep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maktab_\(education\)) or *maktab*, attached to the mosques,[^cite_note-356] The *maktab* provided most of the education and its curriculum was primarily religious and oral.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillward2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8FVsWq31MtMCpgPA146_145–147\]-357] Boys and girls might be taught in separate schools, some of which offered modern secular subjects in the early 20th century.[^cite_note-Millward_PA142-354][^cite_note-benson-355][^cite_note-358] In madrasas, poetry, logic, Arabic grammar and [Islamic law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Law) were taught.[^cite_note-359] In the early 20th century, the [Jadidists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jadid) Turkic Muslims from Russia spread new ideas on education[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363] and popularized the identity of "Turkestani".[^cite_note-364]

In more recent times, religious education is highly restricted in Xinjiang and the Chinese authority had sought to eradicate any religious school they considered illegal.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] Although Islamic private schools (Sino-Arabic schools (中阿學校)) have been supported and permitted by the Chinese government in Hui Muslim areas since the 1980s, this policy does not extend to schools in Xinjiang due to fear of separatism.[^cite_note-VersteeghEid2005-367][^cite_note-368][^cite_note-Relations2005-369]

Beginning in the early 20th century, secular education became more widespread. Early in the communist era, Uyghurs had a choice of two separate secular school systems, one conducted in their own language and one offering instructions only in Chinese.[^cite_note-370] Many Uyghurs linked the preservation of their cultural and religious identity with the language of instruction in schools and therefore preferred Uyghur language schools.[^cite_note-benson-355][^cite_note-371] However, from the mid-1980s onward, the Chinese government began to reduce teaching in Uyghur and starting mid-1990s also began to merge some schools from the two systems. By 2002, [Xinjiang University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_University), originally a bilingual institution, had ceased offering courses in the Uyghur language. From 2004 onward, the government policy has been that classes should be conducted in Chinese as much as possible and in some selected regions, instruction in Chinese began in the first grade.[^cite_note-372] A special senior-secondary [boarding school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boarding_school) program for Uyghurs, the Xinjiang Class, with course work conducted entirely in Chinese was also established in 2000.[^cite_note-373] Many schools also moved toward bilingual Chinese-Uyghur education from 2012,[^cite_note-374] with teaching in the Uyghur language limited to only a few hours a week.[^cite_note-375] The level of educational attainment among Uyghurs is generally lower than that of the Han Chinese; this may be due to the cost of education, the lack of proficiency in the Chinese language (now the main medium of instruction) among many Uyghurs, and poorer employment prospects for Uyghur graduates due to job discrimination in favor of Han Chinese.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377] Uyghurs in China, unlike the [Hui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people) and [Salar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salar_people) who are also mostly Muslim, generally do not oppose [coeducation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coeducation),[^cite_note-378] however girls may be withdrawn from school earlier than boys.[^cite_note-benson-355]

### Traditional medicine

Uyghur traditional medicine is known as [Unani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unani) (طب یونانی), as historically used in the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire).[^cite_note-379] Sir [Percy Sykes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Percy_Sykes) described the medicine as "based on the ancient Greek theory" and mentioned how ailments and sicknesses were treated in *Through Deserts and Oases of Central Asia*.[^cite_note-380] Today, traditional medicine can still be found at street stands. Similar to other traditional medicine, diagnosis is usually made through checking the pulse, symptoms and disease history and then the pharmacist pounds up different dried herbs, making personalized medicines according to the prescription. Modern Uyghur medical hospitals adopted modern medical science and medicine and applied evidence-based pharmaceutical technology to traditional medicines. Historically, Uyghur medical knowledge has contributed to Chinese medicine in terms of medical treatments, medicinal materials and ingredients and symptom detection.[^cite_note-381]

### Cuisine

Main article: [Uyghur cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_cuisine)



Uyghur *[polu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilaf)* (پولۇ, полу)

Uyghur food shows both [Central Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asian_cuisine) and [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_cuisine) elements. A typical Uyghur dish is *polu* (or [pilaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilaf)), a dish found throughout Central Asia. In a common version of the Uyghur *polu*, carrots and mutton (or chicken) are first fried in oil with onions, then rice and water are added and the whole dish is steamed. Raisins and dried apricots may also be added. *Kawaplar* ([Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_language): Каваплар) or *[chuanr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuanr)* (i.e., [kebabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebab) or grilled meat) are also found here. Another common Uyghur dish is *[leghmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leghmen)* (لەغمەن, ләғмән), a noodle dish with a stir-fried topping (*säy*, from Chinese *cai*, [菜](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E8%8F%9C)) usually made from mutton and vegetables, such as tomatoes, onions, green bell peppers, chili peppers and cabbage. This dish is likely to have originated from the Chinese *[lamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamian)*, but its flavor and preparation method are distinctively Uyghur.[^cite_note-382]

Uyghur food (*Uyghur Yemekliri*, Уйғур Йәмәклири) is characterized by [mutton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamb_and_mutton), [beef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beef), [camel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camel_meat) (solely [bactrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactrian_camel)), [chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken_\(food\)), [goose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goose), [carrots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrot), [tomatoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomato), [onions](51258.md), [peppers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capsicum), [eggplant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eggplant), [celery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celery), various [dairy foods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dairy_product) and fruits.

A Uyghur-style breakfast consists of [tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea) with home-baked bread, [hardened yogurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardened_yogurt), [olives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive), [honey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honey), [raisins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raisin) and [almonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almond). Uyghurs like to treat guests with tea, [naan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naan) and fruit before the main dishes are ready.

*[Sangza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangza)* (ساڭزا, Саңза) are crispy fried [wheat flour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat_flour) dough twists, a holiday specialty. *[Samsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsa_\(food\))* (سامسا, Самса) are lamb [pies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pie) baked in a special brick oven. *[Youtazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youtazi)* is steamed multi-layer bread. *Göshnan* (گۆشنان, Гөшнан) are pan-grilled lamb pies. *[Pamirdin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamirdin)* (Памирдин) are baked pies stuffed with lamb, carrots and onions. *[Shorpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chorba)* is lamb [soup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soup) (شۇرپا, Шорпа). Other dishes include *[Toghach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tohax)* (Тоғач) (a type of [tandoor bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tandoor_bread)) and *[Tunurkawab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doner_kebab)* (Тунуркаваб). *Girde* (Гирде) is also a very popular [bagel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagel)-like bread with a hard and crispy crust that is soft inside.

A cake sold by Uyghurs is the traditional Uyghur nut cake.[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384][^cite_note-385]

### Clothing



Doppa maker, traditional Uyghur hats, Kashgar

[Chapan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapan), a coat, and [doppa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doppa), a type of hat for men, is commonly worn by Uyghurs. Another type of headwear, salwa [telpek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telpek) (*salwa tälpäk*, салва тәлпәк), is also worn by Uyghurs.[^cite_note-386]

In the early 20th century, face covering veils with velvet caps trimmed with otter fur were worn in the streets by Turki women in public in Xinjiang as witnessed by the adventurer Ahmad Kamal in the 1930s.[^cite_note-Kamal2000-387] Travelers of the period Sir [Percy Sykes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Percy_Sykes) and Ella Sykes wrote that in Kashghar women went into the bazar "transacting business with their veils thrown back" but mullahs tried to enforce veil wearing and were "in the habit of beating those who show their face in the Great Bazar".[^cite_note-388] In that period, belonging to different social statuses meant a difference in how rigorously the veil was worn.[^cite_note-389]



Uyghur man having his head shaved in a bazaar. Shaving of the head is now seen mostly among the older generations.



Uyghur girl in clothing made of fabric with design distinctive to the Uyghurs

Muslim Turkestani men traditionally cut all the hair off their head.[^cite_note-CrossleySiu2006-390] Sir [Aurel Stein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurel_Stein) observed that the "Turki Muhammadan, accustomed to shelter this shaven head under a substantial fur-cap when the temperature is so low as it was just then".[^cite_note-391] No hair cutting for men took place on the *ajuz ayyam*, days of the year that were considered inauspicious.[^cite_note-392]

### Traditional handicrafts

[Yengisar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yengisar_County) is famous for manufacturing Uyghur handcrafted knives.[^cite_note-Witness-393][^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395] The Uyghur word for knife is pichaq (پىچاق, пичақ) and the word for knifemaking (cutler) is pichaqchiliq (پىچاقچىلىقى, пичақчилиқ).[^cite_note-396] Uyghur artisan craftsmen in Yengisar are known for their knife manufacture. Uyghur men carry such knives as part of their culture to demonstrate the masculinity of the wearer,[^cite_note-397] but it has also led to ethnic tension.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399] Limitations were placed on knife vending due to concerns over terrorism and violent assaults.[^cite_note-400]

### Livelihood



Uyghur women on their way to work in Kashgar, 2011

Most Uyghurs are agriculturists. Cultivating crops in an arid region has made the Uyghurs excel in irrigation techniques. This includes the construction and maintenance of underground channels called *karez* that brings water from the mountains to their fields. A few of the well-known agricultural goods include apples (especially from [Ghulja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yining)), sweet melons (from [Hami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hami)), and grapes from [Turpan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turpan). However, many Uyghurs are also employed in the mining, manufacturing, cotton, and petrochemical industries. Local handicrafts like rug-weaving and jade-carving are also important to the cottage industry of the Uyghurs.[^cite_note-401]

Some Uyghurs have been given jobs through Chinese government affirmative action programs.[^cite_note-402] Uyghurs may also have difficulty receiving non-interest loans (per Islamic beliefs).[^cite_note-403] The general lack of Uyghur proficiency in Mandarin Chinese also creates a barrier to access private and public sector jobs.[^cite_note-404]

### Names

Since the arrival of Islam, most Uyghurs have used "Arabic names", but traditional Uyghur names and names of other origin are still used by some.[^cite_note-Ildikó-Bellér-Hann-2007a-405] After the establishment of the Soviet Union, many Uyghurs who studied in Soviet Central Asia added Russian suffixes to Russify their surnames.[^cite_note-406] Names from Russia and Europe are used in Qaramay and Ürümqi by part of the population of city-dwelling Uyghurs. Others use names with hard-to-understand etymologies, with the majority dating from the Islamic era and being of Arabic or Persian derivation.[^cite_note-407] Some pre-Islamic Uyghur names are preserved in [Turpan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turpan) and [Qumul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qumul).[^cite_note-Ildikó-Bellér-Hann-2007a-405] The government has banned some two dozen Islamic names.[^cite_note-nyt2016-329]

[^cite_note-28]: &#x20;[Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_language): ئۇيغۇرلار, Уйғурлар, *Uyghurlar*, Uyghur pronunciation: [\[ujɣurˈlɑr\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA)[simplified Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters): 维吾尔; [traditional Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 維吾爾; [pinyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinyin): *Wéiwú'ěr*, IPA: [\[wěɪ.ǔ.àɚ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Mandarin)<sup>[\[25\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHahn20064-26)</sup><sup>[\[26\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrompp20057-27)</sup>For the English pronunciation, see [Etymology](#Etymology_of_the_term_"Uyghur")

[^cite_note-turki-100]: &#x20;The term *Turk* was a generic label used by members of many ethnicities in Soviet Central Asia. Often the deciding factor for classifying individuals belonging to Turkic nationalities in the Soviet censuses was less what the people called themselves by nationality than what language they claimed as their native tongue. Thus, people who called themselves "Turk" but spoke Uzbek were classified in Soviet censuses as Uzbek by nationality.<sup>[\[97\]](#cite_note-99)</sup>

[^cite_note-137]: &#x20;This contrasts to the [Hui people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people), called Huihui or "Hui" (Muslim) by the Chinese and the [Salar people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salar_people), called "Sala Hui" (Salar Muslims) by the Chinese. Use of the term "Chan Tou Hui" was considered a demeaning slur.<sup>[\[133\]](#cite_note-136)</sup>

[^cite_note-220]: &#x20;"Soon the great chief Julumohe and the Kirghiz gathered a hundred thousand riders to attack the Uyghur city; they killed the Kaghan, executed Jueluowu, and burnt the royal camp. All the tribes were scattered – its ministers Sazhi and Pang Tele with fifteen clans fled to the Karluks, the remaining multitude went to Tibet and [Anxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guazhou_County)." ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 俄而渠長句錄莫賀與黠戛斯合騎十萬攻回鶻城，殺可汗，誅掘羅勿，焚其牙，諸部潰其相馺職與厖特勒十五部奔葛邏祿，殘眾入吐蕃、安西。)<sup>[\[215\]](#cite_note-219)</sup>

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang2021"Using_qpAdm,_we_modeled_the_Tarim_Basin_individuals_as_a_mixture_of_two_ancient_autochthonous_Asian_genetic_groups:_the_ANE,_represented_by_an_Upper_Palaeolithic_individual_from_the_Afontova_Gora_site_in_the_upper_Yenisei_River_region_of_Siberia_(AG3)_(about_72%),_and_ancient_Northeast_Asians,_represented_by_Baikal_EBA_(about_28%)_(Supplementary_Data_1E_and_Fig._3a)._Tarim_EMBA2_from_Beifang_can_also_be_modeled_as_a_mixture_of_Tarim_EMBA1_(about_89%)_and_Baikal_EBA_(about_11%)."-109]: &#x20;[Zhang 2021](#CITEREFZhang2021), "Using qpAdm, we modeled the Tarim Basin individuals as a mixture of two ancient autochthonous Asian genetic groups: the ANE, represented by an Upper Palaeolithic individual from the Afontova Gora site in the upper Yenisei River region of Siberia (AG3) (about 72%), and ancient Northeast Asians, represented by Baikal_EBA (about 28%) (Supplementary Data 1E and Fig. 3a). Tarim_EMBA2 from Beifang can also be modeled as a mixture of Tarim_EMBA1 (about 89%) and Baikal_EBA (about 11%).". sfn error: no target: CITEREFZhang2021 ([help](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Harv_and_Sfn_template_errors))

[^cite_note-110]: &#x20;Nägele, Kathrin; Rivollat, Maite; Yu, He; Wang, Ke (2022). "Ancient genomic research – From broad strokes to nuanced reconstructions of the past". *Journal of Anthropological Sciences*. **100** (100): 193–230. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.4436/jass.10017](https://doi.org/10.4436%2Fjass.10017). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [36576953](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36576953). Combining genomic and proteomic evidence, researchers revealed that these earliest residents in the Tarim Basin carried genetic ancestry inherited from local Upper Palaeolithic hunter-gatherers, carried no steppe-related ancestry, but consumed milk products, indicating communications of persistence practices independent from genetic exchange.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang2021-111]: &#x20;[Zhang 2021](#CITEREFZhang2021). sfn error: no target: CITEREFZhang2021 ([help](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Harv_and_Sfn_template_errors))

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang2021"Our_results_do_not_support_previous_hypotheses_for_the_origin_of_the_Tarim_mummies,_who_were_argued_to_be_Proto-Tocharian-speaking_pastoralists_descended_from_the_Afanasievo,_or_to_have_originated_among_the_Bactria–Margiana_Archaeological_Complex_or_Inner_Asian_Mountain_Corridor_cultures._Instead,_although_Tocharian_may_have_been_plausibly_introduced_to_the_Dzungarian_Basin_by_Afanasievo_migrants_during_the_Early_Bronze_Age,_we_find_that_the_earliest_Tarim_Basin_cultures_appear_to_have_arisen_from_a_genetically_isolated_local_population_that_adopted_neighboring_pastoralist_and_agriculturalist_practices,_which_allowed_them_to_settle_and_thrive_along_the_shifting_riverine_oases_of_the_Taklamakan_Desert."-112]: &#x20;[Zhang 2021](#CITEREFZhang2021), "Our results do not support previous hypotheses for the origin of the Tarim mummies, who were argued to be Proto-Tocharian-speaking pastoralists descended from the Afanasievo, or to have originated among the Bactria–Margiana Archaeological Complex or Inner Asian Mountain Corridor cultures. Instead, although Tocharian may have been plausibly introduced to the Dzungarian Basin by Afanasievo migrants during the Early Bronze Age, we find that the earliest Tarim Basin cultures appear to have arisen from a genetically isolated local population that adopted neighboring pastoralist and agriculturalist practices, which allowed them to settle and thrive along the shifting riverine oases of the Taklamakan Desert.". sfn error: no target: CITEREFZhang2021 ([help](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Harv_and_Sfn_template_errors))

["Bronze and Iron Age genomes reveal the integration of diverse ancestries in the Tarim Basin"](https://www.cell.com/current-biology/abstract/S0960-9822\(25\)00815-2). *Current Biology*. **35** (15): 3759–3766.e4. 'According to some Uyghur activists, the Uyghurs number around 35 million, however official Chinese statistics put them around 12 million, a far cry from what the indigenous Uyghurs claim.' 'Analyzing historical data from Russian, Turkish, Chinese, and Uyghur sources, Turkish historian Professor Dr. Mehmet Saray expressed in his book *Doğu Türkistan Türkleri Tarihi* \[*The History of Eastern Turkistan's Turks*] that the Uyghurs numbered roughly 24 million within East Turkistan as of 2010.'
